15                                                                                                             15
     F.1.1. Executed calibration and interpretation
16                                                                                                             16

17     The replication program trains a homothetic neural subfamily: a positive value                          17

18   coefficient, a sigmoid consumption-share output, and a projected portfolio-share                          18

19   output. These are neural output parameters in a known invariant representation, not                       19

20   an unrestricted multilayer approximation. The two Adam optimizers have learning                           20

21   rates 0.025 and 0.008; each outer iteration contains three critic updates and one                         21

22   actor update. There are 1,600 outer iterations and seeds 0, 1, 2. All attempted runs,                     22

23   including their coefficient and policy traces, are saved. These calibrations optimize                     23

24   the stationary coefficient equation and then check the resulting coefficient against                      24

25   the matching bequest; the coefficient error is a boundary error in that finite-horizon                    25

26   interpretation, not a boundary condition hard-imposed during these particular                             26

27   updates.                                                                                                  27

28     Table F.I reports quantities generated by the new code. The normalized policy- 28
29   evaluation residual is below 4 × 10−16 in these runs. These results establish that                        29

30   the revised graph and formulas agree in the exactly representable laboratory. They                        30

31   do not establish a broad advantage over a direct analytical calculation. In the exit                      31

32   laboratory, grids with 40, 80, 160, and 320 intervals recover the grid value to within                    32
                                              NBO SUPPLEMENT                                            57

1    3 × 10−15 ; the wrong smooth sequence has scaled residual between 1.984 and 1.999. 1
2    All grid nodes, not a selected interior sample, enter that diagnostic.                                   2

28                                                                                                            28
     F.2.1. A well-defined validation problem at the retained parameters
29                                                                                                            29

30     The numerical calibration uses (S.57) as a finite-horizon matching-bequest test,                       30

31   not as an unqualified infinite-horizon theorem. Choose T > 0, stop wealth on leav- 31
32   ing [ x, x̄ ] with 0 < x < x̄ < ∞, and impose the candidate Kx b /b on the stopping                      32
     58

1    boundary and at T. Consumption shares are in a compact positive interval contain- 1
2    ing 0.0455, and portfolio shares are in a compact interval containing 0.3.                 2

3      For these retained parameters, a > 0 and b < 0. Since c ranges over a compact            3

4    positive interval, there exist 0 < Y < Y < ∞ such that f (c, Y/b) ≤ 0 and f (c, Y/b) ≥     4

5    0 for every feasible c. Indeed, c a Y −a/b tends to zero as Y ↓ 0 and to infinity as Y ↑ ∞. 5
6    Enlarge the interval so that it contains the boundary candidate. The constants Y/b         6

7    and Y/b are respectively lower and upper barriers for the backward utility equation. 7
8    Scalar BSDE comparison therefore keeps every policy utility in this interval. The          8

9    aggregator is Lipschitz there. The smooth homothetic candidate maximizes the               9

10   Hamiltonian and matches all stopping payoffs; Theorem 1 identifies it as the value         10

11   of this specified finite-horizon problem.                                                  11

12     The choice of a matching bequest is an analytical validation device. It does             12

13   not establish that the same candidate is selected by every infinite-horizon recur- 13
14   sive formulation. A separate infinite-horizon application must check its utility- 14
15   process class and transversality, particularly when risk aversion and intertemporal- 15
16   complementarity parameters straddle one (Herdegen et al., 2021). The paper retains         16

17   that economic application, but does not use a scalar root to bypass its selection          17

18   issue.                                                                                     18

19     A critic can enforce bV > 0 through bvξ = exp( Nξ ). With a positive terminal            19

20   target, logarithmic interpolation between bG and the neural output enforces both           20

21   the terminal condition and the power domain. Additional barriers or bounded                21

22   transformations enforce a declared invariant interval. Such transformations are            22

23   stated in the architecture, not imposed retrospectively after a failed evaluation.         23

24                                                                                              24

25                                                                                              25
     F.2.2. Stability and baseline design
26                                                                                              26

27     The recursive experiment in Table F.I tests the corrected sign, coefficient, and         27

28   block graph. It does not compare independent DDPG training trajectories. The               28

29   reviewed manuscript’s constructed oscillating curve and manually entered coordi- 29
30   nates remain in the archival source, but have no evidentiary role in this paper. A         30

31   matched neural comparison must use the same utility process, domain, bequest,              31

32   action restrictions, training budget, and held-out evaluation quantities. It must          32
                                              NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      59

1    report all failed seeds and value-domain violations. Neither a zero-centered target                1

2    nor three successful homothetic calibrations establishes general relative stability.               2

3                                                                                                       3

     F.3.3. Independent two-state reference and identification                                          12

13     The executable reference solves the finite-horizon problem with T = 1, u ∈ [1.2, 3],             13

14   wealth stopped at [0.5, 2], and G (u, X ) = X 1−u /(1 − u) at exit and terminal time. Pa- 14
15   rameters are (r, µ, σX , σu , ϱ, ρ) = (0.02, 0.08, 0.2, 0.08, −0.3, 0.04). The finite action set   15

16   is the product of c/X ∈ {0.02, 0.05, 0.1}, p ∈ {0, 0.375, 0.75}, and θ ∈ {−0.15, 0, 0.15}. 16
17   A four-point Brownian cubature with positive interpolation weights performs back- 17
18   ward induction. The wealth boundary is imposed at every time step; preference                      18

19   overshoots are reflected. This reference defines a finite-action numerical approxima- 19
20   tion, not continuous-action optimality.                                                            20

21     At (u, X ) = (2.1, 1), the original 17 × 25 state grid and 20 time steps give values             21

22   −11.915211 and −11.956983 for k = 1 and k = 5. The 25 × 37 grid and 40 time                        22

23   steps give −11.866768 and −11.908738. Both select (c/X, p, θ ) = (0.1, 0.75, −0.15)                23

24   at that point. The approximately 0.048 refinement change is reported separately                    24

25   from training error. The higher-cost value is weakly lower at every recorded node                  25

26   and time. These original references remain useful consistency checks; Section F.3.4                26

27   supplies actual neural fits and shows how strongly the finite action domain affects                27

28   their economic comparison.                                                                         28

29     The model also specifies what a structural estimation exercise would require. For                29

30   observations Yj = M(St j , at j ; ϑ ) + ε j , let m(ϑ ) denote selected population moments         30

31   of the induced observation process. Local identification requires a rank condition                 31

32   on Dϑ m, after accounting for latent preference states, normalization parameters,                  32
     62

1    and observation noise. Variation of a simulated policy with k alone is insufficient. 1
2    Numerical value and policy errors must additionally be propagated into moment              2

3    errors. The endogenous-preference application is therefore retained as an economic         3

4    model with an explicit internal margin, not used as evidence that its structural           4

5    parameters have already been identified from data.                                         5

6      The next comparison is the retained continuous-action study evidence under               6

7    its original enclosure. The new signed envelope and fresh nested-grid policies are         7

8    reported separately in Section F.8; historical failure counts are not overwritten.         8

9                                                                                               9
     F.3.4. Neural preference policies and the role of the action oracle
10                                                                                              10

11     We compute the original preference-adjustment economy with the cardinal utility,         11

12   shock correlation, reflected preference state, and wealth stopping payoff specified        12

13   above. The main grid has 17 preference points, 25 log-wealth points, and 20 time           13

14   steps over a unit horizon. Adjustment cost is k = 1. The expanded control box is           14

15                                                                                              15
                 m = c/X ∈ [0.02, 0.30],        p ∈ [0, 1.5],     θ ∈ [−0.45, 0.45].
16                                                                                              16

17   The finite-action comparison uses the 175 triples formed by five consumption shares,       17

18   five portfolio shares, and seven adjustment efforts, including all endpoints. This is      18

19   the same constrained economy across the matched finite methods, not a change of            19

20   utility normalization.                                                                     20

21     Two experiments distinguish action representation from action optimization. 21
22   First, the finite-policy study finite study trains two-hidden-layer, width-32 tanh         22

23   critics and categorical actors. At each backward step it computes the complete 175- 23
24   action payoff vector using the current continuation critic. Its exact argmax provides      24

25   a label, and the vector also supplies the actor’s expected action-loss objective. Thus     25

26   the training oracle is exhaustive, even when the final actor is accurate. It is a useful   26

27   neural policy-representation experiment, but not evidence that the actor avoided           27

28   Bellman maximization.                                                                      28

29     Second, continuous-action study trains a continuous-output actor directly                29

30   through its own positive-weight payoff. It never receives an all-action value vector       30

31   or an exact argmax label. The actor receives 160 Adam updates per time level; the          31

32   continuation critic receives 320 Adam updates and up to 300 L-BFGS iterations, with        32
                                              NBO SUPPLEMENT                                                63

1    failed polishing steps rolled back. The actor-free direct comparator optimizes eight                        1

2    independent bounded controls at every state for the same 160 iterations, followed                           2

3    by the same critic fit. Every restart and rejected action query is charged. Neither                         3

4    procedure invokes a Riccati solution, an analytically invariant policy class, or the                        4

5    independently computed reference during training.                                                           5

6                                                                                                                6

7      Finite-action neural policies. The raw finite-policy study actor is the primary                           7

8    neural representation result. Its full dynamic policy loss is below the declared 0.1                        8

9    target for each fixed seed. A top-four shortlist and a separately reported correction                       9

10   map further reduce the loss. The correction map is not part of the raw network. 10
11   The complete finite policy, not only one-step deviations against a fitted critic, is                        11

12   evaluated by independent backward recursion.                                                                12

13                                                                                                               13

     F.5.1. Player-specific differentiation and deviation bounds
11                                                                                                  11

12     Player i’s critic is trained with every policy frozen. Player i’s actor then differenti- 12
13   ates only                                                                                      13

14                               a (ϕi ),sg( a−i )                                                  14
                          −Eν H i i                  [sg(Vi ), sg( DVi ), sg( D2 Vi )].    (S.74)
15                                                                                                  15

16   The updated parameter is ϕi , not the full vector of all players’ parameters. Rival            16

17   actions are detached, and player j’s loss never contributes to player i’s actor gradient. 17
18   An explicit regression test exercises this graph.                                              18

19     For each player, define the unilateral Hamiltonian gap by maximizing Hi over                 19

20   ai while keeping rivals fixed. Applying Theorem 1 to that best-response prob- 20
21   lem bounds its finite-horizon exploitability by 2e Li T Bi + A Li ( T )(2ei + ηi ). In a dis- 21
22   counted finite-state game, Theorem 2 gives (2δi + ϵi )/(1 − qi ). These are player- 22
23   specific bounds; a sum of critic losses is not a substitute. They remain valid without         23

24   uniqueness or convergence of the simultaneous policy-update dynamics, provided                 24

25   the reported profile satisfies the stated errors and best-response comparison hy- 25
26   potheses.                                                                                      26

27     The static Cournot diagnostic makes the distinction visible. With payoffs qi (1 −            27

28   qi − q j ), symmetric Nash output is 1/3 per firm. Joint-profit maximization gives             28

29   a symmetric allocation 1/4. At the latter profile, a firm optimally deviates to 3/8,           29

30   earning a gain of 1/64. A joint objective can therefore be perfectly optimized and             30

31   still fail a unilateral equilibrium test. This arithmetic is included as a mandatory           31

32   regression check.                                                                              32
     70

1    F.5.2. Symmetry and market scaling                                                         1

2                                                                                               2
       Identical initial weights do not prove equilibrium uniqueness or permutation
3                                                                                               3
     equivariance. An equivariant actor can be specified using own capital and a
4                                                                                               4
     permutation-invariant aggregation of rival states. Even then, one must check unilat-
5                                                                                               5
     eral deviations rather than infer equilibrium from architectural symmetry. Multiple
6                                                                                               6
     starting profiles and unsuccessful best-response searches belong in a numerical
7                                                                                               7
     report.
8                                                                                               8
       Under fixed market size, positive constant capital per firm cannot be called
9                                                                                               9
     the competitive limit while total output tends to infinity and the truncated price
10                                                                                              10
     tends to zero. Maintaining that capital entails investment and adjustment costs.
11                                                                                              11
     Under M N = N, positive capital per firm may instead be compatible with a growing
12                                                                                              12
     market, but it describes a different sequence of economies. The original unsupported
13                                                                                              13
     capital table remains in the archive. Section F.5.3 replaces its evidentiary role with a
14                                                                                              14
     fully specified finite dynamic game and independent best-response calculations,
15                                                                                              15
     without asserting convergence of unrestricted neural game training.
16                                                                                              16

17                                                                                              17
     F.5.3. Neural dynamic capital policies and full unilateral deviations
18                                                                                              18

19     For two firms, we retain the capital game with A = 1, P0 = 2, δ = 0.1, σK = 0.15,        19

20   ρ = 0.04, T = 3, investment in [0, 0.8], and capital in [0.05, 1.5]2 . Terminal or joint- 20
21   domain exit pays 0.5Ki . Baseline adjustment coefficients are b1 = b2 = 1; the earlier     21

22   asymmetric experiment has b2 = 1.2. The principal neural grid has 17 × 17 states,          22

23   30 time steps, and 11 actions per player. Positive cubature and interpolation define       23

24   the finite game. Market normalizations M2 = 1 and M2 = 2 are distinct economies,           24

25   not evidence for an uncomputed large-population limit.                                     25

26     At each time and state, the finite-policy study calculation trains separate critics      26

27   and actors. It constructs both full 11 × 11 continuation-payoff matrices. The training     27

28   labels are a pure one-step equilibrium when one exists and a minimum-maximum- 28
29   defect pair otherwise. The actor’s own objective is differentiated with the rival          29

30   action and continuation critic fixed. Thus this study actually trains neural strategic     30

31   policies, but it also pays for an exhaustive joint-action training oracle. Raw policies,   31

32   three-by-three shortlist policies, and any corrected policies are distinct stored arrays. 32
                                             NBO SUPPLEMENT                                              71

1      After freezing the entire profile, an independent backward optimization permits                        1

2    every own action at every state and time against the fixed rival policy. Its value                       2

3    minus the profile payoff is the unilateral exploitability map. This is not a one- 3
4    period test with the approximate continuation held fixed. The raw and final profiles                     4

5    meet the 0.1 absolute-profit target in every principal finite-policy study case; the                     5

6    full-oracle guard is unused in those cases.                                                              6

20     Five state-time nodes across the six neural runs lack a pure one-step equilibrium                      20

21   for the fitted continuation matrices. Their locations and defects are reported in                        21

22   the supplement. A minimum-defect selection at those nodes is not called an exact                         22

23   equilibrium. Nevertheless, the complete dynamic best-response bound remains                              23

24   valid for the resulting profile: its proof does not assume that every fitted one-step                    24

25   game has a pure solution. Moreover, for fixed Markov rivals, randomized or history- 25
26   dependent own deviations cannot improve on the finite Markov best-response                               26

27   value, because each conditional action mixture is bounded by its largest pure-action                     27

28   payoff. The calculation therefore checks such unilateral deviations as well; it does                     28

29   not compute a mixed-strategy equilibrium profile or a continuous-action game.                            29

30     continuous-action study adds actor-free neural continuation fitting and classical                      30

31   backward selection for both market normalizations. The neural comparator allocates                       31

32   480 critic Adam updates per time level rather than finite-policy study’s 320 critic                      32
     72

1    plus 160 actor updates, with the same L-BFGS cap. Counts and timings reveal the                      1

2    difference between nominal update matching and matched wall time. Both methods                       2

3    receive their own independent full dynamic best responses. The earlier six classical                 3

4    game cases, asymmetric costs, alternative tie rules, boundary frequencies, coarse                    4

5    counterexample, and finer-grid results remain in the supplement and raw record.                      5

                                F.6. Scalability and the Diffusion Trace
29                                                                                                        29

30     The original multi-stock consumption-savings specification has additive utility,                   30

31   independent transitions, and no common resource constraint. It decomposes as                         31

32   V (k ) = ∑ j v j (k j ). A separability-aware comparator solves the one-dimensional com- 32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     73

1    ponents, and identical components can be reused. An unreduced sparse grid is not            1

2    an appropriate sole comparator for that economy.                                            2

3      To test a coupled generator, the new laboratory instead uses                              3

4                                                   Z ∞                                          4

5
            dX = ( AX + a)dt + ΣdW,         sup E         e−ρt [− X ⊤ QX − a⊤ a]dt.    (S.75)    5
                                              a     0
6                                                                   √                            6
     The drift is a nonnormal dense matrix A = −0.7I + 0.06Z/ d, where Z is generated
7                                                                                                7
     by the fixed model seed 991 + d. The payoff matrix is Q = diag(1, . . . , 2) + 0.311⊤ /d.
8                                                                                                8
     The code verifies that AQ ̸= QA, so the common independent-coordinate reduction
9                                                                                                9
     of the original benchmark is unavailable. All methods still exploit the declared
10                                                                                               10
     quadratic structure. A Riccati solver is therefore the appropriate strong reference,
11                                                                                               11
     rather than a deliberately unreduced general grid.
12                                                                                               12
       For V = − x ⊤ Px − c0 and a = −Kx, policy evaluation solves
13                                                                                               13

      ( A − K − ρI/2)⊤ P + P( A − K − ρI/2) = −( Q + K ⊤ K ),        c0 = tr(ΣΣ⊤ P)/ρ. (S.76)
14                                                                                               14

15                                                                                               15

16   Hamiltonian improvement gives K = P. A positive semidefinite P is representable             16

17   by a signed sum of squared linear neural units; the actor is linear. The exact block        17

18   laboratory uses a Lyapunov solve for this critic and exact actor improvement. It is         18

19   an exactly representable NBO subfamily and a matrix-identity test, not evidence             19

20   that generic stochastic neural optimization has the same convergence rate.                  20

21     Across dimensions 2, 5, 10, 20, 50 and three stabilizing actor initializations, the       21

22   block iteration takes six steps. The maximum error in P, in operator norm, is below         22

23   10−13 relative to an independent algebraic Riccati solver. The policy-evaluation            23

24   coefficient residual is below 10−12 . On the unit Euclidean ball, the actor gap is          24

25   bounded by ∥K − P∥22 , and the residual bound follows from the operator norm of             25

26   the quadratic coefficient error. The program saves matrices, iteration traces, and          26

27   CPU wall times for both methods. These very short, single-process timings are               27

28   diagnostics and do not establish a fixed-accuracy runtime ranking.                          28

29                                                                                               29
     F.6.1. Trained, coupled nonquadratic capital economies
30                                                                                               30

10                                                                                                10

11                                                                                                11
     dispersion couple the coordinates. Neither the solver nor its comparator calls a
12                                                                                                12

13                                                                                                13

21   gap measurement; it does not supply the value function.                                      21

22     Both networks have two hidden tanh layers of width thirty-two. The critic en- 22
23   forces the terminal payoff exactly. The main design uses three seeds at d = 2, 5, 10, 20,    23

24   600 Adam updates, and batches of 128 independently sampled state–time points. 24
25   Exact automatic differentiation supplies the trace. The held-out set contains 512            25

26   fixed independent points in [0, 1] × [−0.5, 0.5]d . A common diagnostic target is            26

27   residual root mean square at most 0.025 and maximum queried action gap at most               27

28   0.01. The recorded first crossing is not necessarily sustained; final failures are also      28

29   reported.                                                                                    29

31   rather than exact quadratic identities. It also shows why a diagnostic target is not a       31

32   welfare certificate. Simulated policy paths can leave the fitted state box; Euler policy- 32

21     The old twenty-dimensional fitting box is exited by essentially all recorded paths. 21
22   Wider-domain fitting eliminates observed exits in the recorded audits and changes            22

23   both payoff agreement and method differences. Zero observed exits in 512 paths               23

24   is not a zero exit-probability theorem. Likewise, these intervals quantify point- 24
25   wise Monte Carlo uncertainty under a specified Euler scheme. They are neither                25

26   simultaneous confidence statements across all 36 comparisons nor bounds on time              26

27   bias, optimal value, or the global differential residual. The experiment identifies a        27

28   consequential coverage failure and its measured correction without redefining the            28

29   unbounded economic state space as a stopped box.                                             29

30     The one-state consumption certificate has a different evidentiary status. finite- 30
31   policy study independently replays its previously generated complete leaf cover              31

32   with mpmath.iv at 40 decimal digits, without importing the original interval                 32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                77

1    arithmetic implementation. For the stored seed-11 networks, the NBO policy-loss         1

2    bound is about 0.04961 and the direct-HJB bound about 0.04967. Both meet the            2

3    stated threshold. This is an independent interval-arithmetic replay on the previously   3

4    generated complete cover, not independent network training, independent cover           4

5    generation, or a formal machine proof. Together with the new complete three- 5
6    control cover on the two-state preference grid, it demonstrates two different ways      6

7    of operationalizing verification. Neither removes the dimensional cost of covering      7

8    an unrestricted high-dimensional continuous state space.                                8

     F.6.3. Exact and stochastic trace computation
10                                                                                           10

11     Let Σ include any covariance factor and set B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ. For independent probes      11

12   with identity covariance,                                                               12

13
                                                    1 K ⊤                                    13
                                 q = tr( B),    qb = ∑ zk Bzk .                     (S.78)
14                                                  K k =1                                   14

15                                                                                           15

16
     Gaussian probes have variance 2∥ B∥2F /K. If r = d − q/2, then                          16

17
                                  E(d − qb/2)2 = r2 + 41 Var(qb).                   (S.79)   17

18                                                                                           18

19   The extra term depends on the critic. Unbiased trace estimation therefore does not      19

20   give an unbiased squared-residual objective.                                            20

21     Two conditionally independent probe banks repair this particular bias:                21

22                                                                                           22
                     L = (d − qb(1) /2)(d − qb(2) /2),
                     b                                    E[ b
                                                             L | t, x, ξ ] = r2 .   (S.80)
23                                                                                           23

24   With dominated differentiation, its gradient is also unbiased. The sample product       24

25   need not be nonnegative and can have substantial variance, so this is an objective      25

26   identity rather than a guarantee of stable optimization. Exact low-dimensional          26

27   derivatives remain the independent check. If the actor or deterministic term depends    27

28   on the same probes, conditional independence must be re-established; the simple         28

29   identity cannot be reused automatically.                                                29

30     The relevant computational cost depends on the network evaluation and differ- 30
31   entiation cost, Brownian rank, covariance factorization, sample count, optimization     31

32   iterations, and requested accuracy. Forming a dense Hessian involves quadratic          32
     78

1    storage; Hessian-vector products can avoid it. Neither operation count by itself is a        1

2    total-complexity theorem. Active control bounds do not universally create gradient           2

3    discontinuities, and a noisy trace cannot make a correctly projected box action leave        3

4    its box. Feasibility and value-derivative accuracy are separate diagnostics.                 4

5      The independent-bank estimator is also exercised during actual training in the             5

6    coupled ten-state economy. One implementation uses two independent banks of                  6

7    two Gaussian Hessian-vector probes; the matched single-bank ablation squares a               7

8    four-probe estimate. Exact traces are used only for the common held-out diagnostics. 8
9    All three seeds, objective signs, optimization histories, and policy-payoff samples          9

10   are saved. The two training procedures perform similarly at the retained covariance          10

11   scale; unbiasedness alone does not establish a finite-budget advantage. A separate           11

12   Monte Carlo test checks both the product objective and its parameter gradient                12

13   against exact trace expressions with declared tolerances. These calculations validate        13

14   the estimator’s execution without identifying a random training objective with a             14

15   deterministic certificate.                                                                   15

16                                                                                                16

17                                                                                                17
                       F.7. Validated Evaluation and Action Improvement
18                                                                                                18
       The neural Bellman map must account for two distinct approximation decisions:
19                                                                                                19
     which continuation values are passed to the next stage, and how tightly the admis-
20                                                                                                20
     sible action supremum is bounded. This section supplies an explicit output rule
21                                                                                                21
     for the first decision and a signed enclosure for the second. Neither construction
22                                                                                                22
     presumes that a stationary point of the training loss is economically optimal. Both
23                                                                                                23
     apply to the original preference economy and leave its controls, state law, and
24                                                                                                24
     stopping convention unchanged.
25                                                                                                25

26                                                                                                26
     F.7.1. An evaluation rule with a prescribed nodal error
27                                                                                                27

28     Let Tha denote a positive-weight, one-step policy operator on a finite state grid, 28
29   and suppose its continuation weights have sum at most qh ≤ 1. At a given date, let           29

30   w be the continuation vector, a the selected feasible policy, vb the fitted neural critic,   30

31       ea w the computed selected-policy target. We distinguish the computation of
     and T                                                                                        31

1    node, not a table of optimizing labels. For a prescribed tolerance τh > 0, define                 1

6                                                                                                      6

7                                                                                                      7
     by adding two cancelling floating-point numbers. The indexed corrections, their
8                                                                                                      8
     masks, and the uncorrected neural outputs are saved. The deployed continuation
9                                                                                                      9
     is therefore a neural representation with a recorded nodal correction, not a raw
10                                                                                                     10

13                                                                                                     13

15                                                                                                     15
     For a frozen sequence of policies over N stages, with exact terminal data, the guarded
16                                                                                −1    j              16

17                                                                                                     17
     If Nh = T, τh = 0.1h2 , and ηh = o (h), this evaluation perturbation vanishes as h ↓ 0. This
18                                                                                                     18
     statement is a property of the output rule, not a rate for the neural optimizer or an assertion
19                                                                                                     19
     of policy optimality.
20                                                                                                     20

21     The guard gives an implementable meaning to the evaluation component of a                       21

22   vanishing-perturbation condition. It does not supply the action-improvement error,                22

23   the consistency error of a stopped diffusion approximation, or convergence of an un- 23
24   corrected network away from the nodes. Its cost can be substantial: in the numerical              24

25   economy it corrects a large fraction of fitted nodal values. We consequently count                25

26   the correction memory and compare against an actor-free method with the identical                 26

27   guard. The construction is not evidence of neural compression when almost every                   27

28   node is retained.                                                                                 28

29                                                                                                     29
     F.7.2. Signed enclosures for continuous actions
30                                                                                                     30

31     Fix a state-time node and a continuation vector w. Write Qw ( a) for its one-step               31

32   payoff. The action set is the full compact box of the preference economy. Let B = [ℓ, r ]         32
     80

1    be an action rectangle with centre c. A natural interval calculation supplies an upper                        1

2    bound U nat ( B), while independent feasible evaluations supply a lower incumbent                             2

3    L.                                                                                                            3

4         The continuation is bilinear within state cells and continuous across their internal                     4

5    faces. Reflection changes its one-sided slopes. A stopped transition is different:                            5

6    the implementation switches between interpolated continuation and the specified                               6

7    stopping payoff, which need not agree away from boundary grid points. We call B                               7

8    regular when every cubature transition stays entirely inside, or entirely outside, each                       8

9    wealth stopping face. Only on such a box do we use derivative information. All                                9

10   intersected interpolation cells and both sides of reflection folds enter the derivative                       10

11   hull.                                                                                                         11

12        Suppose Gj ( B) = [ g−     +
                               j , g j ] encloses every one-sided partial derivative with respect                  12

13   to action component j on a regular box. The mean-value upper enclosure is                                     13

14                                                                                                                 14

15    U mv ( B) = Qw (c) + ∑ max{ g−                 −                 +                 +
                                   j (ℓ j − c j ), g j (r j − c j ), g j (ℓ j − c j ), g j (r j − c j )}, (S.82)
                                                                                                                   15

16                              j                                                                                  16

17                                                                                                                 17

18   where Qw (c) is an outward point upper bound. The algorithm uses the smaller of                               18

19   this bound and U nat ( B). If g−                                                    +
                                    j > 0, it restricts the supremum to a j = r j ; if g j < 0, 19
20   it restricts it to a j = ℓ j . These are verified reductions to a maximizing face, not                        20

21   decisions based on a sampled derivative. For boxes crossing a stopping switch, the                            21

22   natural union enclosure is retained without this reduction.                                                   22

23                                                                                                                 23

24                                                                                                                 24
          T HEOREM S.8—Signed action cover: Assume that the natural enclosures contain
25                                                                                                                 25
     their respective payoffs, that the derivative hulls contain all one-sided derivatives on each
26                                                                                                                 26
     regular box, and that feasible point values and arithmetic bounds are outward. Start from a
27                                                                                                                 27
     cover of the action box and apply subdivision, reduction to a verified maximizing face, and
28                                                                                                                 28
     removal of a box only when its upper bound is at most L + ε. At any interruption, retain
29                                                                                                                 29
     the upper bound of every unresolved box. Then
30                                                                                                                 30

31                                                                                                                 31
                              sup Qw ( a) ≤ max{ L + ε,           max         U ( B)}.                  (S.83)
32                            a∈A                              B unresolved                                        32
                                           NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    81

1    The bound remains valid when a depth, work, or time budget is exhausted. If r action coordi- 1
2    nates have verified strict derivative signs throughout a regular parent box, maximization    2

3    over that box reduces to a face of dimension at most d a − r.                                3

4                                                                                                 4
       The last statement describes a source of computational improvement, not a
5                                                                                                 5
     dimension-free worst-case bound. Under a K-Lipschitz gradient, a regular stationary
6                                                                                                 6
     box of diameter s has mean-value excess of order Ks2 when its point evaluation
7                                                                                                 7
     error is negligible. Boxes that cross stopping switches, have ambiguous derivative
8                                                                                                 8
     signs, or contain many interpolation cells can remain expensive. Our experiment
9                                                                                                 9
     measures their actual cost and keeps unresolved bounds; it does not assume that
10                                                                                                10
     every box has favorable structure.
11                                                                                                11
       The operational NBO output is now explicit: the frozen policy representation,
12                                                                                                12
     its guarded continuation vector when used, an independently propagated policy
13                                                                                                13
     interval, and a complete-action optimal upper envelope. A nodal lookup policy is
14                                                                                                14
     the object certified in the preference experiment. An off-grid interpolation of that
15                                                                                                15
     policy is a different deployment rule and is evaluated separately. No online search
16                                                                                                16
     is charged to a stored lookup table; constructing that table includes every actor,
17                                                                                                17
     critic, and local-search query.
18                                                                                                18

19                                                                                                19
                           F.8. Cost, Refinement, and Economic Accuracy
20                                                                                                20

21     The preceding applications define the original economic scope of NBO. We now               21

22   evaluate the guarded map and signed cover on the same preference economy, and                22

23   connect the high-dimensional experiment to an absolute economic benchmark. The               23

24   comparison retains every earlier failure. In particular, a new upper envelope does           24

25   not turn an actor-plus-search policy into an actor-only policy, and retraining on a          25

26   finer grid does not repair the off-grid performance of a previously frozen policy.           26

27                                                                                                27
     F.8.1. A tighter certificate for identical frozen policies
28                                                                                                28

29     The reference specification has 17 × 25 state nodes, twenty dates, and the continu- 29
30   ous action box [0.02, 0.30] × [0, 1.5] × [−0.45, 0.45]. The ratios constrain consumption,    30

31   leverage, and preference adjustment in the declared economy. They are economic               31

32   primitives, not bounds on an unconstrained optimum. Active consumption or port- 32
     82

1    folio limits remain meaningful features of this constrained specification; changing                        1

2    them changes the economy.                                                                                  2

3      We freeze the nine previously computed actor-plus-search, direct-plus-search,                            3

4    and search-only policies, including their original seeds. The complete-action upper                        4

5    envelope is recomputed independently of these policies’ lower payoff envelopes. 5
6    The new cover uses a one-step tolerance of 0.0002 and retains every unresolved                             6

7    upper bound. Its output can be shared across policies only because their action set,                       7

8    transition rule, domain, and payoff normalization coincide.                                                8

     F.8.2. The whole-domain refinement failure and its repair                                            23

24     A frozen policy has to be distinguished from a policy refitted on a new grid. The                  24

25   complete historical refinement arrays reveal large losses away from the centre. Their                25

26   maximum positive reference advantages, locations, quantiles, boundary-region                         26

27   summaries, and action saturation rates are retained in the complete numerical                        27

28   archive indexed in Appendix N. The largest historical discrepancy is approximately                   28

29   2.1234 at high risk aversion and wealth close to the lower stopping boundary. Centre                 29

30   values alone do not summarize that experiment. These discrepancies are feasible- 30
31   reference lower bounds on policy regret, not upper bounds for the continuous                         31

32   economy.                                                                                             32

     F.8.3. A model-specific welfare account
5                                                                                             5

6      An absolute utility gap is not a consumption-equivalent percentage when the            6

7    utility exponent is itself a controlled state. We therefore compute a defined com- 7
8    pensating transfer. Hold the saved policy and its state law fixed, leave preference- 8
9    adjustment costs and the stopping bequest unchanged, and add externally financed         9

10   consumption so that flow consumption becomes (1 + e)ct Xt . This intervention            10

11   changes felicity but does not charge its financing to wealth. It is a compensation       11

12   experiment, not another admissible self-financed policy in the original box.             12

13     Let Jeπ be the compensated payoff and U the common optimal upper envelope. 13
14   A verified lower envelope J π
                                 e
                                   ≥ U at every active starting node and date implies 14
15   that this transfer compensates the policy’s loss everywhere in the declared nodal        15

16   economy. We search for a common e using monotonicity of CRRA felicity in con- 16
17   sumption and verify the final inequality by outward propagation. Table F.XII gives       17

18   the resulting upper percentages. These conservative transfers can be economically        18

19   substantial even when the absolute utility bound is below 0.1; the threshold is not      19

20   described as a universally small welfare loss.                                           20

21                                                                                            21

22                                                                                            22
     F.8.4. A global benchmark for the coupled capital economy
23                                                                                            23

24     A comparison of two frozen policies does not indicate how far either is from           24

25   the economic optimum. We supplement the paired simulations by a bound on the             25

26   original, continuous-time, dense nonlinear capital economy. It does not rely on          26

27   a Riccati equation, separability of the optimal policy, a state mesh, or a sampled       27

28   residual.                                                                                28

31   is Y − χ(Y − Y )2 , with χ = 0.03. The drift of Yi is a + κ tanh(( BY )i ) − mi − (σI2 + 31

5                                                                                                  5
     r )/(γσ2 ) when interior. With a no-short-sale constraint and negative excess return,
6                                                                                                  6

     F.9.2. Recursive coefficient and the utility interval
17                                                                                                 17
       For f (c, V ) = ρ[c a (bV )1−a/b − bV ]/a, the same homothetic substitution gives
18                                                                                                 18
                                     ρ
19
                                  0 = [m a K −a/b − 1] + R∗ − m.                                   19

20
                                     a                                                             20

21   Since f c = VX   implies ρm a−1 K −a/b = 1, the first term becomes (m − ρ)/a. Conse-          21

22   quently, m = ψρ + (1 − ψ) R∗    and K = [ρm a−1 ]b/a . The formula assumes the station-       22

23   ary candidate has positive consumption. It is not an infinite-horizon utility-selection       23

24   theorem.                                                                                      24

25     For the finite-horizon matching-bequest test at a > 0 > b, define Y = bV. The               25

26   consumption domain is compact and bounded away from zero. At sufficiently small               26

27   positive Y, c a Y −a/b < 1 uniformly in consumption, so f < 0. At sufficiently large          27

28   Y, the reverse inequality holds and f > 0. Because dividing by negative b reverses            28

29   the order of the utility endpoints, the large-Y constant is a lower utility barrier and       29

30   the small-Y constant is an upper utility barrier. The matching bequest lies between           30

31   them after enlarging the interval. Lipschitz scalar comparison then keeps each                31

32   policy utility inside the interval, on which the aggregator and its utility derivative        32
                                        NBO SUPPLEMENT                                    89

1    are bounded. This verifies the finite-horizon interpretation without claiming an           1

2    unrestricted infinite-horizon result at those parameters.                                  2

5                             F.10. Endogenous-Preference Details                               5

6                                                                                               6
     F.10.1. State restrictions and the reference experiment
7                                                                                               7

8      In the baseline application, the risk-aversion interval lies strictly above one and      8

9    the process is reflected. The reference wealth problem is stopped at positive finite       9

10   bounds. Consumption shares are bounded away from zero, adjustment efforts are              10

11   bounded, and the horizon is finite. These choices bound the flow utility, its relevant     11

12   derivatives away from the terminal-boundary compatibility issue, and the bequest. 12
13   They provide a well-defined finite reference calculation. The numerical reflection         13

14   is a folded reflection of Brownian cubature endpoints; the wealth boundary is              14

15   absorbing with the prescribed payoff. The same terminal and boundary values are            15

16   imposed for both adjustment-cost parameters.                                               16

17     The grid reference operates in y = log X. Its drift is                                   17

18                                                                                              18

19                                                                                              19
                                by = r + p(µ − r ) − m − 12 p2 σX2 ,
20                                                                                              20

21                                                                                              21

22   and its covariance with u is ϱσu pσX . The two Brownian-factor columns can be 22
     written (σu , ϱpσX )⊤ and (0, 1 − ϱ2 pσX )⊤ . Four symmetric cubature directions re- 23
                                  p
23

24   produce this covariance. The correction − p2 σX2 /2 in log wealth is essential; omitting   24

25   it would change the economic diffusion.                                                    25

26     For each action, the backward update is current utility times the time step plus         26

27   the exponentially discounted positive-weight interpolation of next-period value. 27
28   Feasible actions are enumerated on the declared 27-element grid. Because the               28

29   terminal/exit payoff is fixed, raising k weakly lowers each action’s payoff and, by        29

30   backward induction, lowers value at every state and time. The recorded arrays              30

31   permit a direct check of this order. The reported grid-refinement difference is not an     31

32   estimate of unrestricted neural approximation error.                                       32
     90

1    F.10.2. Policy conditions and comparative statics                                            1

2                                                                                                 2
       The consumption marginal utility in reference units is Uc = c̄−1 (c/c̄)−u . Equating
3                                                                                                 3
     it to VX yields c = c̄(c̄VX )−1/u . Adjustment maximizes θVu − kθ 2 /2 and hence
4                                                                                                 4
     uses the projection of Vu /k under a box. Portfolio choice maximizes a quadratic
5                                                                                                 5
     with coefficient X 2 σX2 VXX /2. The projected first-order solution is valid only when
6                                                                                                 6
     this coefficient is negative. These conditions clarify why numerical curvature and
7                                                                                                 7
     feasibility must be checked independently.
8                                                                                                 8
       The cross derivative VuX is an endogenous object. Its sign cannot be inferred from
9                                                                                                 9
     the label “risk aversion” or from a negative shock correlation. Nor can the sign of
10                                                                                                10
     ∂k θ k be inferred by differentiating 1/k while keeping Vuk fixed. In contrast, the value
11                                                                                                11
     monotonicity in k holds policy by policy. At a differentiable optimum the envelope
12                                                                                                12
     formula follows because k appears only in the quadratic running cost. Changing
13                                                                                                13
     the normalization function κ (u) can change both adjustment and portfolio policies
14                                                                                                14
     even while leaving utility curvature in consumption unchanged.
15                                                                                                15
       An observation model is needed for structural estimation. With latent u, uncer-
16                                                                                                16
     tainty in κ, and unknown adjustment or shock parameters, a sensitivity plot for
17                                                                                                17
     one policy component does not prove identification. A practical estimation exercise
18                                                                                                18
     would compute moment derivatives jointly, inspect their rank after normalization
19                                                                                                19
     restrictions, and propagate numerical approximation error into the implied moment
20                                                                                                20
     map. Those are distinct tasks from solving the controlled economy.
21                                                                                                21

                                  F.11. Temporal-Self Deviations
23                                                                                                23

24     For the two-exponential kernel, the component value under constant consump- 24
25   tion share m is Wj ( X ) = mb X b /[bλ j (m)], where λ j (m) = ρ j − b(r − m). A deviation   25

26   to share z for duration ϵ, followed by m, has component value at X = 1                       26

27                                     "                                     #                    27
                                     1 b 1 − e−λ j (z)ϵ                m  b
28                       Jj (z, ϵ) =     z              + e−λ j (z)ϵ           .                  28
                                     b      λ j (z)                  λ j (m)
29                                                                                                29

30   The continuous extension is used if λ j (z) = 0. The deviation gain is ∑ j w j Jj (z, ϵ) −   30

31   ∑ j w j Wj (1), with weights ( β, 1 − β). This is the quantity independently maximized 31
32   in the experiment, rather than a restatement of the equilibrium first-order condition. 32
                                       NBO SUPPLEMENT                                     91

1      At an equilibrium share, the first-order gain is nonpositive for every feasible          1

2    instantaneous share and equals zero at the equilibrium share. A finite-duration self       2

3    can generally choose a slightly different share, so the maximized gain need not be         3

4    exactly zero for positive duration. The required diagnostic is its gain divided by         4

5    duration as duration tends to zero. For β = 1 the ordinary exponential solution is         5

6    recovered.                                                                                 6

7      As κ grows, the kernel becomes increasingly concentrated near the present before         7

8    approaching its long-run component. Taking the instantaneous-deviation limit               8

9    and taking κ → ∞ are conceptually different operations. The finite-κ model in this         9

10   paper explicitly specifies the order: the spike condition is derived for fixed κ, and      10

11   any duration-limit comparative static is taken afterward. This avoids assigning an         11

12   undefined continuous-time meaning to a discrete present-bias multiplier.                   12

13                                                                                              13

14                                                                                              14
                          F.12. Games and Independent Best Responses
15                                                                                              15

16     A fixed rival profile turns each player’s problem into an ordinary controlled            16

17   Markov problem, so the main verification results apply player by player. The               17

18   derivative graph must implement this reduction. During player i’s actor step, every        18

19   rival policy and every derivative of player i’s critic is detached. During player i’s      19

20   critic step, all players’ actions are detached. A sum of these reporting losses can be     20

21   logged, but differentiation of a joint-profit objective is not equivalent to those block   21

22   steps.                                                                                     22

23     The two-firm static diagnostic has best response qi = (1 − q j )/2 when interior. At     23

24   the joint-profit allocation (1/4, 1/4), the deviating firm chooses 3/8 and improves        24

25   profit from 1/8 to 9/64, a gain of 1/64. The own-action derivative is 1/4 at the           25

26   joint optimum. These numbers verify a graph-level distinction; they are not a              26

27   computation of the dynamic capital game.                                                   27

28     For the dynamic problem, a reported equilibrium should include an indepen- 28
29   dent solution of each fixed-rival best-response problem, or an upper bound on              29

30   the unilateral Hamiltonian gap over the entire verification domain. A finite list of       30

31   unsuccessful deviations does not supply such an upper bound. Multiple initializa- 31
32   tions, asymmetric profiles, boundary actions, and the market-size normalization are        32
     92

1    part of the same experiment. The specification in the main text retains the dynamic       1

2    Cournot application but does not invent a dynamic Nash table from the static              2

3    arithmetic.                                                                               3

4                                                                                              4

5              F.13. Proof and Implementation of the Continuous-Action Envelope                5

6      We use the notation of the main text’s budget-safe envelope proposition. The            6

7    nodal economy is fixed before verification. Its terminal and stopping payoffs, prim- 7
8    itive coefficients, interpolation abscissae, and compact action box are part of the       8

9    object being bounded. This is stronger than checking many action samples and              9

10   weaker than verifying the continuous-state diffusion without a discretization ac- 10
11   count.                                                                                    11

12                                                                                             12
     F.13.1. Complete-cover invariant and backward proof
13                                                                                             13

14     At a given state and time, the initial action box covers the entire feasible set. Bisec- 14
15   tion replaces one closed box by two closed boxes with the same union. Their shared        15

16   face is harmless. Induction on the number of subdivisions preserves coverage. A           16

17   pruned box is retained logically through the inequality u B ≤ ci + τ, where u B is        17

18   an outward upper enclosure on that whole box, and ci is a lower enclosure at a            18

19   feasible incumbent. If the incumbent later increases, the previous pruning inequality     19

20   remains true. Consequently every feasible action is either in an unresolved box or        20

21   in a pruned box with payoff at most the current threshold. Taking the maximum of          21

22   the threshold and all unresolved upper bounds dominates the action supremum,              22

23   whether or not the supremum is attained. Termination on a numerical budget does           23

24   not change this argument.                                                                 24

25      At N, the terminal inequalities are given. If Vn∗+1 ≤ Un+1 , positivity gives 25
26   Tn Vn∗+1 ≤ Tn Un+1 ≤ Un . If Ln+1 ≤ Vnπ+1 , the same argument gives Ln ≤ Tnπn Ln+1 ≤ 26
27   Tnπn Vnπ+1 = Vnπ . Feasibility implies Vnπ ≤ Vn∗ . This proves the main envelope inequal- 27
28   ity. Substochasticity further gives                                                       28

29                                                                                             29
                                 ∥ Tn w − Tn z∥∞ ≤ qn ∥w − z∥∞ .
30                                                                                             30

31   Applying this inequality to Un − Tn Vn∗+1 yields the stated excess recursion and its      31

32   product-sum expansion. The proof uses qn > 0, not a stationary contraction assump- 32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                  93

1    tion. Outward addition is used for the pruning threshold and final subtraction. The      1

2    implementation records a requested tolerance and a retained optimization bracket         2

3    separately. A budget-exhausted bracket is never replaced by that tolerance. Q.E.D.       3

4                                                                                             4

5
     F.13.2. Interpolation, reflection, stopping, and arithmetic                              5

6      For a cell with coordinates (u0 , u1 ) × (y0 , y1 ), the continuation is               6

7                                                                                             7
               Iw(u, y) = (1 − s)(1 − t)w00 + s(1 − t)w10 + (1 − s)tw01 + stw11 ,
8                                                                                             8
                                       u − u0          y − y0
9                                   s=         ,    t=         .                              9
                                       u1 − u0         y1 − y0
10                                                                                            10

11   For fixed u, this is affine in y, so its extremes on a subrectangle occur at the two y   11

12   endpoints. At either endpoint it is affine in u, so the extremes occur at the two u end- 12
13   points. Applying this argument to every intersected cell proves the corner enclosure. 13
14   Nonnegative interpolation weights also permit interval nodal inputs: evaluating the      14

15   corner expressions outward encloses every admissible nodal realization. A separate       15

16   valid enclosure evaluates the rectangle center and adds cell-slope bounds over every     16

17   intersected cell. Slopes are computed from stored nodal differences, not estimated       17

18   from random derivative samples.                                                          18

19     Preference reflection is piecewise affine. Its extrema on an interval occur at the     19

20   interval endpoints or a crossed folding point. The allowed one-step movement is          20

21   checked to exclude unhandled multiple folds. For log wealth, boxes fully inside          21

22   the domain use interpolation, boxes fully outside use the prescribed clipped exit        22

23   payoff, and boxes crossing an exit face use the union of both. On the stored grid the    23

24   boundary nodes equal the exact prescribed payoff; at an off-grid exit the analytic       24

25   payoff need not equal its nodal interpolant. The union construction avoids an            25

26   unjustified global smoothness or Lipschitz assumption across that switch.                26

27     The implementation inherits the archived outward elementary and transcen- 27
28   dental routines. Products, quotients, powers through logarithm and exponential,          28

29   and endpoint rounding are enclosed. The logarithm’s input is bounded below by            29

30   0.02; the preference denominator is separated from zero because u ∈ [1.2, 3]. The        30

31   recorded control model treats the stored binary64 primitive coefficients and ab- 31
32   scissae as exact real inputs. The evidence also tests agreement with the inherited       32
     94

1    training evaluator on the finite action grid. Such a regression check is useful for        1

2    implementation consistency but is not the proof of global action coverage. That            2

3    proof is the maintained union invariant and the interval inclusion property. The           3

4    arithmetic account is conditional on the specified floating-point execution model,         4

5    not a formal verification of hardware, libraries, or the Python interpreter.               5

6                                                                                               6

7    F.13.3. A common upper envelope for multiple policies                                      7

8      The optimal upper array depends on the economy, not on which feasible incum- 8
9    bent produced the lower search values. Thus the completed upper array can be               9

10   reused for any frozen feasible policy on the same state, time, and continuous-action       10

11   domains. We separately propagate each policy’s lower and upper payoff arrays and           11

12   subtract its lower array from the common optimal upper array. Costs are reported           12

13   both as shared verification and as the cost of an individual policy evaluation. Shar- 13
14   ing a verifier does not make its cost vanish. It also does not justify transferring the    14

15   upper array to a changed wealth box, action constraint, utility normalization, time        15

16   step, or interpolation rule.                                                               16

17                                                                                              17

18                                                                                              18
                   F.14. Separating Numerical and Continuous-Economy Error

14     The proposition can use local regularity, coupling, barrier, or weak-approximation                  14

15   estimates to bound its defects; it does not claim that those hypotheses hold au- 15
16   tomatically for a fitted critic near a stopped boundary. In the present two-state                     16

17   computations, action coverage is operationally bounded on the nodal economy. 17
18   The continuous-state/time defects remain explicitly uncomputed. Nested grids                          18

                         F.15. Strategic Deviations and Missing Pure Stages
19                                                                                              19

20     For a fixed Markov rival profile in a finite-horizon finite-state/action game, condi- 20
21   tion on any history. The own player’s conditional payoff from a randomized current         21

22   action is a convex combination of pure-action payoffs with the same continuation           22

23   best-response values. It cannot exceed the largest such payoff. Backward induction         23

24   therefore bounds every randomized and history-dependent unilateral policy by the           24

25   Markov pure-action best-response recursion. Outward propagation then provides              25

26   a bound over this full deviation class, not merely the particular local deviations         26

27   observed during actor training.                                                            27

28     Existence of a pure equilibrium in a fitted one-step game is a different matter. 28
29   If none exists, minimum-defect selection produces a feasible profile, not an exact         29

30   equilibrium. The independent dynamic best response still evaluates that profile            30

31   correctly. The following records give every missing pure stage in the six principal        31

32   finite-policy study neural runs, including time index, state, and minimum maximum          32
                                             NBO SUPPLEMENT                                               97

1    stage defect. Indices begin at zero. The six runs have 6 × 30 × 225 = 40,500 active                       1

2    state-time nodes; the five recorded exceptions are not excluded from their final                          2

3    dynamic bounds.                                                                                           3
