          APPENDIX G: S TOCHASTIC H ESSIAN O BJECTIVES AND C OMPLEXITY                          7

8                                                                                               8
       Let B = Σ⊤ D2 vΣ, a symmetric matrix in Brownian-factor space. For Gaussian
9                                                                                               9
     probes, Isserlis’ identity gives E(z⊤ Bz) = tr B and Var(z⊤ Bz) = 2 tr( B2 ) = 2∥ B∥2F .
10                                                                                              10
     Averaging K independent probes divides the variance by K. Rademacher probes
11                                                                                              11
     instead have variance 2 ∑i̸= j Bij2 /K. The variance depends on the chosen covariance
12                                                                                              12
     factor and probe law, both of which must be specified.
13                                                                                              13
       Write qbj = q + ϵ j , with conditionally mean-zero independent errors. Then
14                                                                                              14

15                                                                                              15
               E[(d − qb1 /2)(d − qb2 /2)] = (d − q/2)2 + 14 E[ϵ1 ϵ2 ] = (d − q/2)2 .
16                                                                                              16

17                                                                                              17

18   Using one bank twice would retain its variance term. In the scalar Gaussian example,       18

19   a single-bank squared residual has expectation 1 − h + 3h2 /4, minimized at h = 2/3,       19

20   whereas the exact residual (1 − h/2)2 is minimized at h = 2. The independent               20

21   product restores the latter population objective. The saved Monte Carlo diagnostic         21

22   uses 400,000 pairs and records both empirical estimates; it does not claim that the        22

23   sampling error is identically zero.                                                        23

24     The core implementation uses exact Hessians to make the covariance contraction           24

25   independently testable. A Hessian-vector implementation can replace it without             25

26   changing the conditional-independence requirement. The cost of a Hessian-vector            26

27   product depends on the network graph, not just the state dimension. Total cost also        27

28   includes sampling, optimization, domain coverage, action maximization, and stop- 28
29   ping at an agreed error level. A quadratic-neural representation with a Lyapunov           29

30   solve has different approximation and optimization costs from a generic multilayer         30

31   network. The quadratic laboratory is explicitly the former; the new dense nonlinear        31

32   experiments actually train generic networks and Hessian-vector estimators.                 32
     102

1                       G.1. Implemented Error Accounts and Arithmetic                            1

2                                                                                                 2
       The implemented map returns a candidate and a verification record, rather than
3                                                                                                 3
     assigning authority to an optimizer’s exit code. In the differential consumption
4                                                                                                 4
     economy, the record identifies the binary64 weights, both stopping faces, all dyadic
5                                                                                                 5
     leaves, global consumption maximization, and the error bounds for the continuous
6                                                                                                 6
     diffusion. In NDU it instead identifies the finite state–action–time model, its positive
7                                                                                                 7
     transition weights, and time-indexed evaluation and improvement errors. The
8                                                                                                 8
     dynamic-game record fixes the entire rival policy before an independent backward
9                                                                                                 9
     best-response solve. An unchecked continuous-action or time-discretization term is
10                                                                                                10
     represented as unavailable in the machine-readable output, not as zero.
11                                                                                                11
       For finite time levels, let δt = ∥vt − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ and ϵt = ∥ Tt vt+1 − Tta vt+1 ∥∞ . If
12                                                                                                12
     both maps are β t -Lipschitz, the triangle inequality gives
13                                                                                                13

14                                                                                                14

15     ∥vt − Vta ∥ ≤ δt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vta+1 ∥,   ∥vt − Vt∗ ∥ ≤ δt + ϵt + β t ∥vt+1 − Vt∗+1 ∥.     15

16                                                                                                16

17   Summing the two inequalities yields the regret account. The same recurrence with             17

18   a player’s unilateral gap bounds the gain from a dynamic deviation when that                 18

19   player’s policy evaluation is exact. Accounts must be checked at each time level:            19

20   a time-zero bound need not dominate a later-time bound when discounting and                  20

21   errors differ. The coarse-game regression exercises this distinction and verifies that       21

22   a missing pure equilibrium is reported rather than silently treated as exact.                22

23                                                                                                23

24                                                                                                24
     G.1.1. Full-domain interval implementation
25                                                                                                25

26     An interval [l, u] encloses an exact real quantity. Each elementary operation ex- 26
27   pands its rounded endpoints with the adjacent binary64 number. Affine layers split           27

28   positive and negative weights and include a conservative dot-product forward-error           28

29   allowance. The arithmetic contract requires binary64 round-to-nearest arithmetic,            29

30   the stated dot-product error bound, and absence of exceptional overflow or flush- 30
31   to-zero behavior. Stored parameters denote their exact binary64 real values; the             31

32   verifier does not assert an unrecorded decimal model identity.                               32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                103

1      For an exponential argument x, range reduction gives r = x/2s with |r | ≤ 1/8. A      1

2    degree-fourteen Taylor polynomial has remainder bounded by                              2

3                                                                                            3
                               e|r| |r |15 /15! < 2(1/8)15 /15! < 2−80 .
4                                                                                            4

5    Interval Horner evaluation and repeated squaring enclose e x . The identity tanh x =    5

6    1 − 2/(e2x + 1) then supplies tanh values and derivative channels 1 − tanh2 x and       6

7    −2 tanh x (1 − tanh2 x ). Powers of two and the range-reduction domain are checked      7

8    in the code.                                                                            8

9      For logarithms, exact binary decomposition gives x = 2k m, with 1 ≤ m < 2. Writ- 9
10   ing z = (m − 1)/(m + 1),                                                                10

11                                                                                           11
                                24
                                    z2j+1                      2|z|51
12                   log m = 2 ∑           + R,       | R| ≤          2
                                                                        < 2−72               12
                               j =0
                                    2j + 1                   51(1 − z )
13                                                                                           13

14   for the outward-enclosed range |z| < 0.334. The same expansion encloses log 2. Nei- 14
15   ther construction presumes correctly rounded library tanh or logarithm functions. 15
16   The saved test suite checks transcendental endpoints against eighty-digit arithmetic,   16

17   differentiates the trained network independently, and checks all certificate hashes     17

18   and leaf adjacencies. These tests supplement, but do not replace, the arithmetic        18

19   contract.                                                                               19

20     The scalar action objective h(c) = log c − pc has second derivative −1/c2 . If C      20

21   encloses both the actor and its maximizing alternative, strong concavity on their       21

22   joining segment gives                                                                   22

23                                                                                           23
                 h(c∗ ) − h(c) ≤ h′ (c)(c∗ − c) − (c∗ − c)2 /(2C2 ) ≤ C2 h′ (c)2 /2.
24                                                                                           24

25   The argument includes constrained endpoints. Taking the minimum with the natu- 25
26   ral interval action-gap enclosure markedly reduces verification cost, without using     26

27   a sampled gradient as a bound. Residual targets are decided on every leaf; reaching     27

28   a maximum subdivision depth does not change a failed target into a pass.                28

29                                                                                           29

30
     G.1.2. Application-specific hypotheses                                                  30

31     The stopped consumption diffusion is uniformly nondegenerate on its compact           31

32   domain; controls and coefficients are bounded and its endpoint data are continuous. 32
     104

1    The smooth networks and strictly positive consumption map meet the regularity            1

2    and log-domain requirements of the verification argument. Both stopping-face             2

3    errors and the full action interval are checked. The upwind reference error is not a     3

4    premise of its continuous certificate.                                                   4

5      The NDU computation is a positive-weight, finite-action, finite-horizon economy        5

6    with prescribed discrete stopping and folded preference transitions. Positivity,         6

7    all terminal and wealth-face values, and every finite action are evaluated. The          7

8    continuous reflected PDE’s corner compatibility, continuous-action coverage error,       8

9    and diffusion approximation error are not inferred from these tests. Expanding a         9

10   feasible box changes the constrained economy; densifying a fixed box does not. The       10

11   sensitivity record keeps these operations separate.                                      11

12     The dense capital computation uses bounded nonlinear drift in log coordinates,         12

13   bounded positive consumption fractions, constant diffusion, and finite horizon. Its      13

14   flow has at most linear log-state growth and its terminal payoff at most quadratic       14

15   growth; these are integrable under the declared bounded-drift diffusion. The fitted      15

16   box is a sampling domain, not a state constraint. Its sampled differential residuals     16

17   do not check comparison barriers or uniform errors on Rd . Euler payoff estimates        17

18   carry both sampling error and unbounded-state/time-discretization issues not             18

19   covered by the reported Monte Carlo standard error.                                      19

20     The dynamic capital game is a fully enumerated finite approximation with               20

21   stopped joint boundaries. Stage equilibria and dynamic fixed-rival best responses        21

22   are numerical statements about that model. The continuous-control best-response          22

23   comparison assumptions in the main theorem remain logically distinct. The re- 23
24   tained homothetic recursive-utility and temporal-self examples use their stated          24

25   utility domains, bequests, and integrability conditions; initial evaluation study does   25

26   not relabel them as unrestricted neural experiments.                                     26

27                                                                                            27

30                                                                                            30

32   now specify an observation model under which the controller uses the history of          32
                                         NBO SUPPLEMENT                                      105

1    observed capital rather than latent economic shocks. The model assumes contin- 1
2    uous noiseless observation of every capital coordinate, known diffusion and drift              2

3    coefficients, knowledge of the controller’s own actions, and independent private               3

4    randomization. It does not assume that a finite set of noisy observations reveals              4

5    Brownian increments.                                                                           5

24     Private randomization in (S.84) is essential for this exact equivalence: the right- 24
25   inverse term alone has rank d, whereas the implemented clipping acts separately                25

26   on d + 1 drivers. The construction does not recover the original unobservable null             26

27   component path by path. It replaces that component by an independent one with                  27

28   the correct law. Nor is the resulting controller a deterministic function of the state at      28

29   a single decision date; its memory and independent randomization are part of its               29

30   definition. The integrated drift in the proposition is an observation-model primitive. 30
31   A sensor grid, drift quadrature or measurement error would need a further transfer             31

32   allowance and is not silently treated as exact.                                                32

12   is G-adapted. Since it equals ΣWt , it is a G-martingale: conditional expectation            12

14   A = Σ⊤ (ΣΣ⊤ )−1 and a unit null vector n, the process AM + nB0 is a continuous               14

15   G-local martingale starting at zero. Its bracket is Id+1       t, because AΣ and nn⊤ are     15

16   complementary orthogonal projections and [ M, B0 ] = 0. The multivariate Lévy                16

19     This argument also establishes causality. At a decision date, the already observed         19

20   drift integral determines the preceding innovation increment and hence the next              20

21   internal state. The next action is then fixed before the next Brownian increment. The        21

22   held-action state equation is uniquely solvable on that cell. Induction identifies the       22

23   joint law of physical state, internal state, actions, and recovered innovations with         23

24   the prescribed innovation controller. The independent null component supplies the            24

25   correct law when clipping is applied separately to d + 1 coordinates; it does not            25

26   recover the original latent null component. The exact observation result is separate         26

27   from the finite measurement implementation below.                                            27

10   binds the parameter and arithmetic files by hashes. Exponential and logarithmic            10

11   enclosures use range reduction and polynomial remainder bounds rather than                 11

12   an assumed accuracy of a library transcendental. Its mathematical conclusion is            12

13   conditional on the stated binary64 arithmetic contract; high-precision and derivative      13

14   tests check the implementation but are not a formal machine proof. The construction        14

15   does not estimate a global Lipschitz constant from samples.                                15

16                                                                                              16

17
     I.1.1. Controlled comparison and failures                                                  17

18     We use seeds 11, 29, and 47, each with 256 prespecified jittered collocation points. 18
19   Eight outer rounds have a common cap of 800 L-BFGS inner iterations. NBO allo- 19
20   cates each round sixty iterations to evaluation and forty to improvement; direct           20

21   neural HJB uses the same critic and the global action rule in all one hundred itera- 21
22   tions. A joint-loss ablation differentiates the sum of squared residual and negative       22

23   Hamiltonian through both blocks. Line searches use different numbers of closures,          23

24   so this is a matched iteration cap, not an assertion of equal floating-point work. 24
25   Closure counts and elapsed times are saved.                                                25

26     An independent positive-rate upwind policy-iteration solver uses continuous- 26
27   action maximization within each drift region. Reference grids have 320, 640, 1,280,        27

28   and 2,560 intervals. Value and derivative comparisons use the finest grid; its re- 28
29   finement differences are not substituted for a rigorous discretization bound. The          29

30   continuous certificate instead checks the neural functions directly.                       30

31     All three NBO and direct-HJB runs attain the declared interval-verification thresh- 31
32   olds. The joint-loss ablation does not: a residual near one half is accompanied by         32
                                               NBO SUPPLEMENT                                             115

17   large value and action errors. Width, depth, and update-allocation variations are                            17

18   retained in the supplement, including unfavorable outcomes. The direct method                                18

19   is a strong comparator: these results do not establish that carrying an actor is uni- 19
20   formly faster or more accurate. They establish an end-to-end neural solve with an                            20

21   independently computable welfare bound, and identify a concrete failure of joint                             21

22   differentiation. Fitting and full-domain verification are separately timed because                           22

23   their computational burdens are different.                                                                   23

               I.2. Independent Scalar Reference: Equations and Complete Diagnostics
26                                                                                                                26
     I.2.1. The scalar reference and its residual account
27                                                                                                                27

28     The scalar reference isolates policy evaluation and policy improvement in a                                28

29   nonlinear member of the coupled-capital family. It uses the three stored scalar actors                       29

30   selected in the original experiment and the analytical anchor, without retraining. The                       30

31   four state–time–domain specifications are (401, 256, 6), (801, 512, 6), (1601, 1024, 6)                      31

32   and (1601, 1024, 8), where the last coordinate is the half-width L of the log-capital                        32
     116

3    input hashes before computation and verifies the input hashes again afterwards.            3

22   For a fixed policy, omit the maximization and use its stored feedback action. The             22

23   diagnostic files retain the maximum residual at every time step, the linear residual          23

24   of every Howard evaluation, every iterate difference, and the iteration count. The            24

25   original stopping rule is an iterate difference below 10−11 , with a cap of 100 itera- 25
26   tions. A separate Howard check evaluates ∥Tk (vk ) − vk ∥∞ /h, where Tk solves the            26

27   linear system for the greedy policy selected from vk .                                        27

28     The significance of these residuals can be stated directly. Let ve be the exact solution    28

29   of the same finite-grid backward equations, with the same frozen policy (or the same          29

30   Bellman operator), terminal data and boundary data, and let v be the approximate              30

31   sequence whose defect is r. In exact arithmetic, the fixed-policy matrix has positive         31

32   diagonal, nonpositive off-diagonal entries, and row sums at least h−1 + ρ after               32

13   The archived propagation diagnostic substitutes the observed floating-point resid- 13
14   uals in this formula. These residuals are not outward-rounded enclosures, so that          14

15   substitution is a numerical diagnostic rather than a certified bound. The estimate         15

16   concerns the finite-grid equations with the prescribed boundary data; it does not          16

17   include consistency, state interpolation or boundary bias.                                 17

18     The tables report all four grids, the five initial states y0 ∈ {−1, −0.5, 0, 0.5, 1},    18

19   the policies’ action-bound frequencies, and changes in values, optimal actions and         19

20   policy losses. Initial-state values are linearly interpolated from nodal time-zero         20

21   values. Bound frequencies use an absolute tolerance of 10−12 and count equally             21

22   weighted interior state–time nodes; they are not occupation probabilities. The             22

23   first two grid comparisons halve both h and δ. Increasing L from 6 to 8 at fixed           23

24   (ny , nt ) = (1601, 1024) changes δ from 0.0075 to 0.01. This comparison measures          24

25   joint domain and spacing sensitivity, not boundary truncation error in isolation. 25
26   Full common-domain changes and their locations accompany the central-region                26

27   summaries.                                                                                 27

28     The scalar calculation supplies an independent finite-grid benchmark for non- 28
29   linear feedback, with a reproducible algebraic error account. In the 10-, 20- and          29

30   50-dimensional economies, cross-sectional dispersion, the coupling matrix and the          30

31   covariance structure remain economically active. Moreover, this scalar benchmark           31

32   evaluates Markov feedback, whereas the continuous-time deployment theorem                  32
                                               NBO SUPPLEMENT                                             119

3    sions rely on the separate multidimensional policy comparisons and certification                            3

4    arguments.                                                                                                  4

      is their maximum. The assessment includes loading the already selected policy, rebuilding constants,
7                                                                                                              7
      candidate occupation paths, both continuation banks, reverse derivatives, interval arithmetic and out-
8                                                                                                              8
      put. Future updates count mathematically active continuation transitions; padded rows additionally
9     disclose vectorized inactive arithmetic. This work is reported in addition to the primary algorithms’    9

10    complete stopping and independent payoff-assessment costs.                                               10

12   APPENDIX L: A N O UTWARD A LGEBRAIC E RROR A CCOUNT FOR THE S CALAR                                       12

13                                             C OMPARATOR                                                     13

14     Fix the declared spatial nodes, common spacing ∆y, time step h, action endpoints,                       14

15   and terminal and boundary data. Each stored binary64 input denotes an exact real                          15

16   in this finite-grid problem. Let η = (σI2 + σC2 )/2, d0 (y) = a − η + κ tanh y, and define                16

17   the forward and backward slopes p f = (vi+1 − vi )/∆y and pb = (vi − vi−1 )/∆y. 17
18   The action-dependent upwind Hamiltonian is                                                                18

19                                                                                                             19
                          ζ
20     Hi (m; v) = log m − m2 + max{d0 (yi ) − m, 0} p f + min{d0 (yi ) − m, 0} pb . (S.92)                    20
                          2
21                                                                                                             21

22   The positive-drift and negative-drift branches are each concave in m. Their fea- 22
23   sible intervals are, respectively, [ℓ, min{u, d0 (yi )}] and [max{ℓ, d0 (yi )}, u], when                  23

24   nonempty. On either branch, the unconstrained positive critical point solves                              24

25   1/m − ζm = p j . The Howard update clips this point to the branch interval and                            25

26   compares the two resulting objective values. Thus the sign of the drift selects the                       26

27   appropriate difference operator throughout maximization.                                                  27

28     The floating-point Howard solve proposes values and actions. A separate out- 28
29   ward calculation encloses the Bellman residual, including the error in the proposed                       29

30   maximizer. For a concave branch H on [ a, b] and any m◦ ∈ [ a, b],                                        30

31                                                                                                             31
           max H (m) ≤ H (m◦ ) + max{0, H ′ (m◦ )( a − m◦ ), H ′ (m◦ )(b − m◦ )}.                     (S.93)
32        m∈[ a,b]                                                                                             32
     132

1    Interval evaluations of log, tanh, the slopes, and the arithmetic operations enclose             1

2    the right-hand side. Interval uncertainty in d0 (yi ) is handled by enlarging each               2

3    branch interval before constructing its upper bound. An arbitrary globally feasible              3

4    action supplies a lower bound for the maximized Hamiltonian. Combining the two                   4

5    bounds encloses                                                                                  5

               vk,i − vk+1,i            v      − 2vk,i + vk,i−1

     The artifact stores every proposed value and action, the boundary arrays, the action

     endpoints, and the outward residual bound at every time step.

       P ROPOSITION S.15—Algebraic error in the declared scalar boundary problem:
12                                                                                                    12
     Fix finitely many ordered spatial coordinate labels and a common positive difference spacing
13                                                                                                    13
     ∆y, h > 0, ρ ≥ 0, η ≥ 0, compact action intervals [ℓk , uk ] with 0 < ℓk ≤ uk < ∞, and
14                                                                                                    14
     finite terminal and boundary data. Let ve denote the exact solution of the backward Bellman
15                                                                                                    15
     equations (S.94) with zero residual. Let v have identical terminal and boundary data and
16                                                                                                    16
     residuals rk,i . For any Rk ≥ maxi |rk,i |, define EN = 0 and

20   Then the exact finite-grid solution exists uniquely, and ∥vk − vek ∥∞ ≤ Ek at every time step.   20

22     P ROOF : For a fixed action m, write the discrete generator at an interior node as             22

23                                                                                                    23
                       ( Lm w)i = λi− (m)(wi−1 − wi ) + λi+ (m)(wi+1 − wi ),

29   Both rates are nonnegative. Each frozen-policy matrix therefore has nonpositive                  29

30   off-diagonal entries and diagonal dominance at least h−1 + ρ after the boundary                  30

31   values are moved to the right-hand side. This is the M-matrix comparison property                31

32   used below.                                                                                      32
                                                 NBO SUPPLEMENT                                       133

1      For completeness, existence can be established at each backward step by uni- 1
2    formizing these finitely many bounded rates. Choose Λ ≥ maxi,m {λi− (m) + λi+ (m)}. 2
3    When Λ > 0, Lm = Λ( Pm − I ), where Pm has nonnegative entries and row sums                             3

4    one before the fixed boundary values are substituted. The Bellman equation is                           4

5    a fixed-point equation whose dependence on the unknown interior values has                              5

6    supremum-norm Lipschitz constant at most Λ/(h−1 + ρ + Λ) < 1. The fixed-point                           6

7    theorem gives a unique solution. If Λ = 0, the equation is pointwise and its co- 7
8    efficient h−1 + ρ is strictly positive. Backward recursion starts from the specified                    8

9    terminal values.                                                                                        9

10     To establish the error bound, suppose ∥vk+1 − vek+1 ∥∞ ≤ Ek+1 and set ei = vk,i −                     10

11   vek,i . If M = maxi ei > 0, the maximum occurs at an interior node i, because the                       11

12   boundary error is zero. Nonnegative rates give ( Lm e)i ≤ 0 for every feasible m. 12
13   Writing f i (m) = yi + log m − ζm2 /2, it follows that                                                  13

14                                                                                                           14
                         sup{( Lm vk )i + f i (m)} − sup{( Lm vek )i + f i (m)} ≤ 0.
15                        m                                 m                                                15

     Subtract the exact and approximate Bellman equations at this node. The preceding
17                                                                                                           17
     inequality implies
18                                                                                                           18
                         −1                −1                                    −1

21
     Interchanging vk and vek gives the same bound for the largest negative error. Equa- 21

22
     tion (S.95) follows, and induction from EN = 0 completes the proof.                            Q.E.D.   22

       The implementation evaluates this recurrence outward under the inherited arith-
24                                                                                                           24
     metic contract. It controls algebraic solution error for a specified finite-grid bound-
25                                                                                                           25
     ary problem. The separate grid, domain, and deployed-policy mesh comparisons

     measure the remaining numerical sensitivities.

1    every initial-date node,                                                                          1

                                0 ≤ ve0,i − ve0,i ≤ ∥v0F − v0T ∥∞ + E0F + E0T .
3                                                                                                      3

4
     In particular, identical stored initial-date values reduce the upper bound to E0F + E0T .         4

5                                                                                                      5

6      P ROOF : At each backward step, use the common uniformization constant from                     6

7    the preceding proof for both action sets. The resulting Bellman maps are mono- 7
8    tone in continuation and current interior values. Enlarging the maximization set                  8

9    increases the map pointwise. The contraction fixed points therefore inherit this                  9

10   order; backward induction from the common terminal data gives veF ≥ veT . The                     10
